Intel syntax:
- `FADD ST(0), ST(i)`? Actually instruction `FADD ST, ST(i)`? Intel manual: `FADD ST(0), ST(i)` adds ST(0) to ST(i) and stores in ST(i)? Let's check.
For x87, most operations are non-commutative for subtraction, but addition commutative. Syntax often in Intel: `FADD ST(1), ST` means `ST(1) = ST(1) + ST(0)`. The destination is the first operand in Intel. `FADDP ST(1), ST` means add ST(0) to ST(1), pop, result at ST(0).
AT&T reverses operands: source first, dest second. Thus `fadd %st, %st(1)` means dest st(1), source st. So result st(1)=st(1)+st. Good. `faddp %st, %st(1)` same plus pop. Good.

Let's construct final code carefully line by line:

```
__asm__ volatile (
    "movw %[x1], %%di\n\t"
    "movw %[A], %%bx\n\t"
    "movw %[n], %%cx\n\t"
    "jcxz 9f\n\t"
    "1:\n\t"
    "movw %[y1], %%si\n\t"
    "xorw %%ax, %%ax\n\t"
    "2:\n\t"
    "fldl (%%di)\n\t"
    "fldl (%%bx)\n\t"
    "fmull (%%si)\n\t"
    "faddp %%st, %%st(1)\n\t"
    "fstpl (%%di)\n\t"
    "addw %[sz], %%bx\n\t"
    "addw %[sz], %%si\n\t"
    "addw %[sz], %%ax\n\t"
    "cmpw %[nbytes], %%ax\n\t"
    "jb 2b\n\t"
    "addw %[sz], %%di\n\t"
    "decw %%cx\n\t"
    "jne 1b\n"
    "9:\n"
    "movw %[A], %%bx\n\t"
    "movw %[y2], %%di\n\t"
    "movw %[n], %%cx\n\t"
    "jcxz 8f\n\t"
    "3:\n\t"
    "movw %[x2], %%si\n\t"
    "xorw %%ax, %%ax\n\t"
    "4:\n\t"
    "fldl (%%si)\n\t"
    "fldl (%%bx)\n\t"
    "fmull (%%di)\n\t"
    "faddp %%st, %%st(1)\n\t"
    "fstpl (%%si)\n\t"
    "addw %[sz], %%bx\n\t"
    "addw %[sz], %%si\n\t"
    "addw %[sz], %%ax\n\t"
    "cmpw %[nbytes], %%ax\n\t"
    "jb 4b\n\t"
    "addw %[sz], %%di\n\t"
    "decw %%cx\n\t"
    "jne 3b\n"
    "8:\n"
    : /* no outputs */
    : [x1] "m" (x1),
      [A] "m" (A),
      [n] "m" (n),
      [y1] "m" (y_1),
      [x2] "m" (x2),
      [y2] "m" (y_2),
      [nbytes] "d" ((unsigned)(n) * sizeof(DATA_TYPE)),
      [sz] "i" (sizeof(DATA_TYPE))
    : "ax", "bx", "cx", "si", "di", "cc", "memory"
);
```

The 8087 reads the memory operand at the time of the ESC instruction; the CPU hands the address to the 8087 during the ESC instruction. The CPU then continues to the next instruction, but the 8087 has already captured the address? I think yes. If not, modifying si before the FPU reads it could be bad. Is that possible? Important! Let's recall 8087 protocol: The 8086 ESC instruction with a memory operand supplies the effective address and performs a bus cycle (dummy read?) to allow the 8087 to capture the operand. The CPU waits for the bus cycle, then proceeds. The 8087 reads the memory operand using its own bus cycles *after* taking the address from the CPU? Actually for `fldl`, the 8087 needs to read 8 bytes from memory; the CPU is not involved? The 8087 is a coprocessor that monitors the address/data bus during the instruction stream. On the ESC instruction, the CPU emits the ModRM and if memory operand, a dummy read cycle; the 8087 latches the address and then controls the bus to fetch the operand while the CPU continues. The address is latched before the CPU proceeds. So later modifying si doesn't affect the latched address. Thus okay.
